## founder-distribution

Added 2026-07-28. This skill shipped with no entry here — the only skill in the kit dense with
empirical market claims and no grounding, while its own "Honest-warrant discipline" section demands
three warrant tiers and ends "A rule that no evidence could break is not a rule." This section closes
that. Thirteen load-bearing claims were traced to primary sources; **four do not survive as written**
(rows 2, 3, 5 and 7), and row 9 is actively contradicted by the literature it assumes. Two further
figures that travel with this material — the Facebook density threshold and the 2–3× willingness-to-pay
multiplier — are ruled do-not-ship at the end of the section.

| # | Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | Win the first 100 on a surface that delivers value in one zero-configuration interaction; accounts, alerts and personalization are second-stage | `founder-distribution` → "a single zero-configuration interaction" | **PRIMARY** on the ordering, **SOFTEN** on universality | Dated sequencing holds across reference-data products, and the gap is years: CoinMarketCap was a top-200 global site (TechCrunch, 1 May 2018) and shipped its first mobile app with watchlists *that same day*, five years in. Etherscan's own 2015→2024 timeline lists no accounts or notifications milestone at all; its watch-list doc (17 Dec 2019) opens "All the features above are only available for our registered users." Zillow's Zestimate drew 1M visitors in three days, ungated. **Boundary:** every supporting case is a *reference-data* product whose value is a public fact a stranger verifies instantly. Glassdoor gated contribution from day one and exited at $1.2B, and GoodUI's Thomasnet A/B test found a registration wall produced a **+1135% relative lift in signups** (with −18% engagement, which is why the team refused to ship it). State the conditional, not the universal. |
| 2 | Several analytics tools scaled to millions with **no push feature at all** | `founder-distribution` — no anchor (claim cut) | **WRONG as written — replace** | No source supports it. CoinMarketCap shipped watchlists in its year-5 app; Etherscan documented account-gated email alerts in Dec 2019; Similarweb and Ahrefs both sell alerting today. The defensible claim is the *sequencing* one: alerts arrived at year 4–5 as a retention layer on an existing base, never before scale. Note the symmetry — the push-retention literature that would contradict the skill (Airship's 63M-user benchmark, Kahuna's 45.3% vs 20.4%) is published by push vendors selling push, so **neither direction is independently measured.** |
| 3 | Almost every company got its first thousand users through **one** channel, not a portfolio of five | `founder-distribution` — no anchor (claim cut) | **WRONG as written — SOFTEN to the sequencing claim** | The frequency claim is a universal quantifier with no counting behind it; no dataset counts channels per company at any stage. Traction's actual sentence carries a precondition the skill drops: "we suggest focusing on one at a time, **but only after you've identified a channel that seems like it could actually work**", and Bullseye deliberately runs ~3 channels in parallel at the test stage. The book makes no first-thousand claim — co-author Justin Mares assigns that stage to Phase I: "giving talks, writing guest posts, emailing people you have relationships with, attending conferences", four of the nineteen channels at once. The widely-cited "70% from one channel" traces to a growth-SaaS vendor page with no study, sample, or stage definition. Survivorship bias is a named, unanswered objection (Gudema). |
| 4 | A gate is accretive **only when all four** hold: no network effects on the gated side · screens on fit · non-transferable admission · live capacity constraint | `founder-distribution` → "Treat these as pressure, not as a rule with four boxes to tick" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** as a heuristic; **not** a measured regularity | Per condition: **(a) SOFTEN** — Bluesky confirms (3.14M→5.1M in the month after opening), but Gmail, Clubhouse and Facebook all gated network-effects products and grew. **(b) UNTESTED** — no case anywhere measures fit or retention differences between gated and ungated cohorts. **(c) WRONG** — contradicted by the three largest gated user bases in the case set. **(d) JUDGMENT-CALL** — the only condition with a clean record, but a real capacity limit explains why a gate is *tolerable*, not why it is *accretive*. The errors are asymmetric: every false negative (Gmail, Clubhouse, Robinhood) is a gate the rule forbids that worked. |
| 5 | The famous gate that worked was a capacity constraint coupled to a measurement program | `founder-distribution` → "Two gates are cited as the canonical success and they did not do the same thing" | **PRIMARY** on Superhuman, **WRONG** as a general lesson | Superhuman's gate was a mandatory 30-minute human onboarding (90 minutes early on), founder-run at first, and it existed so the PMF survey could run on a controlled cohort — instrumentation, not scarcity. It was also revenue-positive: each Onboarding Specialist carried ~$650k ARR against $60–130k loaded cost. **But Gmail — the most-cited gate in software — violates two of the four conditions and worked anyway.** Invites were bearer tokens with a liquid secondary market: Qin Lei (SMU) counted **63,378 invitations trading for $393,027 in three months**, 5,454 sellers across 42 countries. Google's own people call the exclusivity unintentional: "it had a side effect… it was a little bit unintentional" (Harik, TIME). The transferable lesson is not any single condition — **both gates were removed** (Gmail 14 Feb 2007; Superhuman student carve-out at PMF, then three years of engineering to self-serve). A gate is scaffolding with a scheduled demolition date. |
| — | — the hardware number beside it | `founder-distribution` → "a rack of second-hand hardware" | **RESOLVED 2026-08-18 — figure cut as instructed** | Was live as "three hundred second-hand Pentium IIIs" and load-bearing (it is the evidence that Gmail's gate was a capacity constraint rather than exclusivity), with no source. The figure is widely attributed to Paul Buchheit, but no primary text was ever pinned to it, so it was never citable as a measurement. The 2026-08 pass took this row's own prescribed exit and dropped it to "a rack of second-hand hardware" — the mechanism claim survives, the unsourced count does not. Do not re-add the number without a primary text. |
| — | — the condition the rule is missing | — | **ADD** | **Duration.** Every gate that measurably hurt did so through *time on the list*, not gate shape. Superhuman's own waitlist operator reports it converted **~3x worse than the concurrent live funnel**, halving for each additional year queued (3% → 1.5% → 0.8%). The four-condition rule has no variable for this and waves through Bluesky's year-long gate. Any shipped version needs a fifth clause: open within a bounded window measured in months. |
| 6 | A threshold on what *enters* a stream beats human curation at the exit | `founder-distribution` → "Fix the entry bar before you staff the exit" | **JUDGMENT-CALL**; drop the comparative framing | No study anywhere compares filter-at-ingestion against curate-at-exit and reports a winner. The measured adjacent base is clinical alarm management, and it is graded only "moderate" by AHRQ (of 17 studies: 10 QI initiatives, 5 case studies, 1 quasi-experimental). It also cuts both ways: an Apple News audit found **human curation beat the algorithm** on source diversity, while Peukert et al. found the algorithm beat human editors on clicks. The best-designed study on the premise — a micro-randomized trial on the *Drink Less* app — found each notification gave a 3.5x near-term lift and **"no overall difference in time to disengagement"** across arms. Transfer risk is real: a missed ICU alarm is a death, nurses cannot unsubscribe, and every clinical study measures alarm *count*, not response quality. Defensible reduced form: a stream that admits non-actionable items degrades response to the whole stream, and exit-side triage does not recover it (Google SRE: "Every page should be actionable"). |
| 7 | The users hurt worst by flooding are those who took the highest-friction action to arrive | `founder-distribution` — no anchor (claim cut) | **WRONG — cut it** | No supporting evidence exists; a deliberate search returned only vendor marketing. The nearest real literature points the *opposite* way — sunk-cost and investment effects predict higher-effort users are more tolerant and better retained, not more fragile. |
| 8 | Modest virality with long retention beats high virality with 30-day churn | `founder-distribution` → "modest virality and long retention beats one with high virality and 30-day churn" | **PRIMARY as arithmetic; SOFTEN as an empirical law** | Skok's model is unambiguous: users grow as `K^(t/ct)`, "Unless you have a Viral Coefficient that is greater than 1, you will not have true viral growth", and cycle time dominates K. The comparison follows *given* two stated assumptions — K is not constant (Chen: saturation and platform dependence push it under 1) and K is retention-gated ("New users won't create a viral factor >1 in their first visit"). That is a model result, not an observation; say so. Also note the framing is largely a false choice: "Most of the time, you see viral factors that are 0.2 or 0.3 or below", and Skok's own conclusion is the hybrid model — sub-1 loops **require** a paid or content source to function. |
| 9 | Fix retention **before** optimizing acquisition | `founder-distribution` → "Fix retention before optimizing acquisition" | **JUDGMENT-CALL — actively contradicted; the weakest claim in the skill** | The Ehrenberg-Bass Institute contradicts the ordering on evidentiary grounds: "The idea of plugging the leak became popular with theorists who sold the idea to practical marketers, who believed, **without any evidence**, that retention is cheaper than acquisition." Seufert: churn is more expensive later-stage than at onboarding, and a worse retention curve can be the better business. For social/network products the mechanism is bidirectional — retention is not stably measurable until acquisition supplies liquidity, so "fix retention first" is incoherent for that class. Balfour's joint-constraint framing is the replacement: a 10% flattened curve is uninterpretable without knowing whether your acquisition motion can reach the volume it implies. **What survives** is a claim about interpretation, not work order: acquisition performance carries no information about retention, so acquisition wins are not evidence of fit. |
| 10 | PMF is a number: survey "how would you feel if you could no longer use this" and build for the **40%** who answer very disappointed | `founder-distribution` → "PMF is a number — but not the number you think" | **SOFTEN — keep the question, drop the threshold as a gate** | Ellis discredits the number in the sentence that introduces it ("The Startup Pyramid", startup-marketing.com 2009 — now 404, readable only via archive.org): "**Admittedly this threshold is a bit arbitrary**, but I defined it after comparing results across nearly 100 startups." No published dataset, no control group, no operationalized outcome; MeasuringU went looking and reported "we could not find any peer-reviewed papers describing research with the PMF item." Ellis's own sample description is inconsistent ("nearly 100" vs "100s"). **Documented failure modes:** churned users are excluded *by construction* (his distribution rule surveys only people who used the product twice in the last two weeks), so a product with catastrophic retention and a devoted residue scores well; segment gaming is step one of the famous success case (Superhuman went 22%→33% before shipping any product change); at n=50 the margin of error is ±13%, so "41%" and "29%" are the same measurement; and it asks a *hypothetical* question. **Superhuman scored 22% — below the bar — and reached $100M+ ARR.** Use it as a same-segment trend line, never report a score without n, segment, activity filter and churn treatment, and treat >40% as necessary-not-sufficient (Ellis's own 2010 concession). |
| 11 | Hand-recruit the first hundred; it is research that happens to collect users | `founder-distribution` → "Hand-recruit the first hundred" | **PRIMARY** for the prescription, **SOFTEN** for the causal claim | Graham states it unhedged: "The most common unscalable thing founders have to do at the start is to recruit users manually. Nearly all startups have to." The mechanism most retellings drop is the part that matters — "Over-engaging with early users is not just a permissible technique for getting growth rolling. For most successful startups it's a necessary part of the feedback loop that makes the product good." Learning first, acquisition second: success is not "100 users", it is "the product changed as a result." **What is not primary** is the implied causation — three cases chosen after the fact. Garber (Forbes, Aug 2013) names it: "There's a reason why the author calls out Facebook, Stripe, and AirBnB as examples: they were successful!" **Three things to carry that the popular version omits:** Graham's own precondition ("if the market exists"), an **exit trigger** (he gives none, and the documented counter-case is a company that committed to a call with every user — "Guess what? It didn't scale"), and the fact that Stripe's "Collison installation" (a *closing* technique) and Stripe's manual backstage merchant-account signup (a Wizard-of-Oz *delivery* trick) are two different behaviours that most write-ups merge. |
| 12 | Interview about past behavior, never about your idea | `founder-distribution` → "What did you do the last time this happened?" | **PRIMARY** for the rule; the real warrant is stronger than the usual citation, but the usual **magnitude is WRONG** | The Mom Test states the rule ("Ask about specifics in the past instead of generics or opinions about the future") but is a self-published handbook with no data in it. The actual support is two peer-reviewed literatures nobody cites: the **intention–behaviour gap** (Sheeran & Webb 2016 — a medium-to-large change in intention produces only a small-to-medium change in behaviour, *d+* = .36; the mechanism is the "inclined abstainer", which is exactly the interviewee who sincerely says yes and never buys), and **hypothetical bias in stated preference** (Murphy et al. 2005). **The folk figure is wrong:** founders repeat that people overstate willingness to pay by 2–3x; the meta-analysis found a median hypothetical-to-actual ratio of **1.35** across 83 observations — and the authors write "only 1.35" precisely to correct this belief. Do not quote 2–3x. The better argument is the one the same paper supplies: the distribution "has severe positive skewness", so most answers are mildly inflated and a minority wildly so, and you cannot tell which you have. Variance, not a correctable bias you could divide out. |
| — | — the apparent conflict with the PMF survey | `founder-distribution` → "PMF is a number" vs "never about your idea" | **RESOLVE, do not collapse** | The skill endorses both "ask about the past, not the hypothetical future" and an item that asks "how would you feel if you could no longer use this" — which looks like a contradiction and mostly is not. Both literatures above measure people forecasting a behaviour they do **not** currently perform; the Ellis item is asked of people **already using the product**, reporting on an existing behaviour by imagining its removal. Citing *d+* = .36 as a refutation of the Ellis item misapplies the source. The real defect is different and worse for the skill: the item elicits an affective reaction and then uses it as a proxy for retention, and **that inferential leap has never been validated**. Resolution to state explicitly: the Mom Test rule governs *discovery* with non-users, where the intention–behaviour gap bites hardest; the Ellis item governs *retention diagnostics* with existing users. Different stages, different populations. Neither justifies the 40% number. |
| 13 | Counts that only go up are not metrics | `founder-distribution` → "A metric earns its place by naming an action" | **SOFTEN** — right examples, wrong rule | The three named examples (waitlist size, registered users, total messages sent) are Eric Ries's own and are PRIMARY-grounded. But the source rule is *decision-usefulness*, not monotonicity: "They might make you feel good, but they don't offer clear guidance for what to do… Let's say you have 10,000 [hits]. Now what?" Croll & Yoskovitz's test is four-part, of which rate-or-ratio is one. A weekly-active count can go down and still fail all four. **Exception clause worth adding** (Cohn, Gothelf): a cumulative count is admissible as a leading indicator pre-PMF, and inadmissible the moment it appears in a goal, a board deck, or a fundraise. **Provenance:** the primary text is Ries's tim.blog guest post, 19 May 2009 — citing *The Lean Startup* (2011) as the origin is wrong. |

### Missing mechanisms

Four mechanisms the thirteen claims never reach. The first is a deliberate exclusion argued back in;
the rest are genuine gaps.

- **Paid acquisition under a payback-period constraint.** The skill excludes growth tactics, but the *gate* is a judgment tool, not a tactic: Skok's modelled cohort P&L shows "profitability is anemic if the time to recover CAC extends beyond 12 months", against a ~3:1 LTV:CAC floor. Without it a founder reads claims 1–13 and concludes paid is disreputable; the evidenced position is that paid is fine iff CAC is recovered inside ~12 months.
- **Programmatic / template-page SEO.** Not a tactic — a data-model decision made before the first user, which is exactly claim 1's territory. Chen names the mechanism and the cases (Yelp, Stack Overflow); Zapier is the canonical B2B instance. The mechanism is well-evidenced; every available traffic figure is a third-party SEO-tool estimate, so cite the mechanism, not the numbers.
- **Pricing and packaging as the distribution decision.** Claim 1 legislates the shape of the entry surface and stops before the paywall that decides whether anyone converts. OpenView's 2022 benchmarks put freemium at 5% free→paid conversion with 65% self-serve share, against free trial's 17% and 20% — a ~3x swing on a decision the skill never mentions. Self-reported founder survey; still the largest openly published dataset on the question.
- **Bottom-up B2B adoption and its ceiling.** The skill's unit of analysis is the individual user throughout. In B2B the individual is not the buyer, so the first-surface claim (1) and the channel-concentration claim (3) are right for the first 100 and silently wrong at 1,000. a16z documents the bottom-up-only motion stalling around $20M ARR.

### Claims the primary evidence contradicts

1. **"Several scaled to millions with no push feature at all"** — no supporting instance exists.
2. **"Almost every company… one channel"** — the book it leans on prescribes parallel testing first and addresses a later stage entirely.
3. **The non-transferability condition** — Gmail, Clubhouse and Robinhood, the three largest gated user bases available, all violate it.
4. **"The users who took the highest-friction action drown first"** — unevidenced, and the nearest literature points the other way.
5. **"Fix retention before optimizing acquisition"** — Ehrenberg-Bass calls the underlying belief evidence-free.
6. **The Facebook "80% of Harvard before expanding" density threshold**, if any version of this skill leans on it — no primary source; expansion to Columbia, Stanford and Yale began about one month after the 4 Feb 2004 launch. The one peer-reviewed paper built on that rollout measures students' later earnings, not whether gating helped Facebook grow. Do not ship the figure.
7. **"People overstate willingness to pay by 2–3x"** — the folk figure behind the interview rule. The meta-analysis puts the median at **1.35**, and says "only 1.35" to correct exactly this. Do not repeat it.

### Do-not-cite

The SEO tier restating the Sean Ellis 40% rule (learningloop, stackmatix, koji.so, formbricks,
surveysparrow, zonka, ideaplan, fitsignal, prelaunch) — several assert "hundreds of startups" without
the arbitrary admission, and at least one asserts "that threshold isn't arbitrary", which the origin
post directly contradicts. `aggregator` + `false_authority`. Likewise the "70% of customers from one
channel" figure and the "1 channel to $50M, 2 to $100M" heuristic: both circulate with no study,
sample, or stage attached.

## Harness facts — subagent nesting and concurrency (corrected 2026-08-18)

A post-apply recheck caught the kit asserting the OPPOSITE of the documented default, in two
skills at once. Both are now corrected against the primary source. Recorded here because a
confidently wrong structural claim about the harness the kit runs in is the worst kind of error it
can ship — every design decision downstream inherits it.

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| A subagent CAN spawn subagents — **three layers below the main conversation by default**; `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_SUBAGENT_SPAWN_DEPTH=1` turns nesting off | `designing-agents` → "three layers deep by default" · `dispatching-parallel-agents` → "three layers deep by default" | **PRIMARY** | Claude Code docs, Subagents — https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents : *"By default, a subagent can spawn subagents of its own, up to three layers below the main conversation."* and *"Set `1` to turn nesting off."* |
| At the depth limit the harness **withholds the `Agent` tool** from the child — enforcement is structural, not advisory | `designing-agents` → "withholds the `Agent` tool" | **PRIMARY** | Same page: *"At the depth limit, Claude Code withholds the `Agent` tool from every subagent except a fork, so a subagent at the limit does its delegated work itself and returns one summary."* This is the citable example of enforcing a cap in code rather than in the prompt. |
| **20 concurrent subagents** by default; the 21st fails and the error tells the model not to retry | `dispatching-parallel-agents` → "concurrency cap of 20 running subagents" | **PRIMARY** | Same page: *"By default, when 20 subagents are running in a session, spawning another with the Agent tool fails with `Concurrent subagent limit reached`, and the error tells Claude not to retry."* Configurable via `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_CONCURRENT_SUBAGENTS`. Depth and concurrency are different levers. |
| ~~"Every shipped harness that ships sub-agents caps nesting at depth 1"~~ · ~~"nesting is zero levels deep"~~ | — | **REMOVED — WRONG AS WRITTEN** | **Do not re-add.** Both shipped in the 2026-08-18 apply pass and both contradict the documented default above. The universal quantifier ("every shipped harness") was the tell: it was never checked against the one harness that could be checked in seconds. |

## get-shit-done

The skill's centre of gravity is **MEP — the minimum evolvable product**, which is stage 4. Since
the 2026-08-22 pass the skill is the whole project spine (carve → recon → build → reckon), so the
three earlier stages carry their own primary rows below. Rule of thumb for this section: the
*ordering* claims are Steinhardt, the *long-run agent behaviour* claims are Anthropic's harness
write-up, the *concurrency* claim is Cognition, and the slice/budget constants are recipe knobs.

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **MEP — "you don't just need a minimum viable product. You need a minimum evolvable product"** | `get-shit-done-4-reckon` → "the minimum evolvable product" | **PRIMARY** | Y Combinator, *How To Get Your First Users* — https://youtu.be/0kARDVL2nZg (uploaded 2026-01-14, 341s). Transcript pulled with `yt-dlp` and read raw. The concept is stated as an **addition** to MVP, not a harsher MVP — both halves ("survives contact", "can evolve") are load-bearing. |
| A first version **does not have to be perfect** — the licence for the clamp's rough-edge rule | `references/mep-gate.md` → "Reversibility licenses imperfection" | **PRIMARY** | Same talk: *"It's freeing to know that the product will change a lot, so it doesn't have to be perfect from the start."* This is why the skill's strictness lands on evidence of survival and evolvability and **never** on polish. |
| Product evolution is **path-dependent on early adopters** | was anchored in `get-shit-done` at the phrase "Name the real-world pressure"; the bullet was CUT in the 2026-08-18 corpus pass as a second run of Step 1's drift-in attribution under a different name. The claim itself is unchanged and still true — re-anchor it if the bullet ever returns. | **PRIMARY (claim live, anchor retired)** | Same talk: *"product evolution is path-dependent on what the early adopters wanted"*, with the Tesla Roadster as the worked case. |
| Finding first users is **a search problem, not a persuasion problem** — the licence for "zero users is a search failure, not a build failure" | `references/mep-gate.md` → "Zero users is a search failure" | **PRIMARY** | Same talk. The channel work itself belongs to `founder-distribution`; this skill produces the named-candidate list and stops. |
| Ship at a **measured** hit rate; the misses need a named exit | `get-shit-done-3-build` → "give the tail a named exit" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | The rule is a recipe knob, not a claim about the world: measure the fraction of real cases handled, then define what the user sees on the rest. Deliberately carries **no number** — the supporting figure we found lives only in a private corpus, and the constitution forbids citing an internal path from a shipped file. If a public figure is wanted later it needs its own PRIMARY row with a real URL. |
| Health-gated cutover: the last working version keeps serving until the new one answers a real request | `references/mep-gate.md` → "with the last working version still up" | **JUDGMENT-CALL / CANONICAL** | Convergent default across deploy platforms (boot new instance → health probe → re-point traffic; failure cancels the deploy). Vendor-neutral common knowledge; no citation needed. |
| A working code-editing agent is **~300 lines and three tools** — *"an LLM, a loop, and enough tokens"*, the rest *"Elbow grease"* — and the author's own challenge to the sceptic: *"I bet it's a lot farther than you think."* This is the licence for the skill's opening thesis that hard-looking projects are usually smaller than they look | `get-shit-done` → "Elbow grease" | **PRIMARY** | Thorsten Ball (Amp / Sourcegraph), *How to Build an Agent (The Emperor Has No Clothes)* — https://ampcode.com/notes/how-to-build-an-agent . Read as a claim about *this class* of project (agent inner loops), not a universal law; the skill states the counterweight in the same breath by making stage 2 go check. |
| Order components by **information gained per unit time**, not easiest-first — the easiest-first default wastes the easy work when the hard part turns out infeasible (*"The work on the easy parts was mostly wasted"*) | `get-shit-done-1-carve` → "Order by what fails fastest" | **PRIMARY** | Jacob Steinhardt, *Research as a Stochastic Decision Process* — https://cs.stanford.edu/~jsteinhardt/ResearchasaStochasticDecisionProcess.html . |
| **"De-risk all components (to the extent feasible), then execute"** — the stage-1→2 ordering law | `get-shit-done-1-carve` → "de-risk all components, then execute" | **PRIMARY** | Same essay (its "Basic Pattern"). |
| The four **confidence buckets** (~95 / ~90 / ~65 / ~30%), quoted verbatim | `get-shit-done-1-carve` → "murky view of the path" | **PRIMARY** | Same essay. Steinhardt's own caveat travels with them: *"These numbers are all completely made up and in practice you won't be able to estimate things so well"* — they are subjective bands for sorting, never measurements, which is why the skill uses them only to rank and to set a research budget. |
| **Ceilings** — substitute a cheating version of the hard component and wire the whole path anyway: a working system proves a real implementation would work, a failing one saves building it | `get-shit-done-1-carve` → "with the hard parts faked" | **PRIMARY** | Same essay. |
| **Baselines** — *"complicated methods often underperform simple baselines"*, so a baseline that clears the check deletes the slice | `get-shit-done-1-carve` → "the dumb off-the-shelf version" | **PRIMARY** | Same essay. |
| Long-running agents fail two documented ways: they *"tended to try to do too much at once"* (one-shotting), and later *"a later agent instance would look around, see that progress had been made, and declare the job done."* | `get-shit-done-3-build` → "declare the job done" | **PRIMARY** | Anthropic (Applied AI), *Effective harnesses for long-running agents* — https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents . That was cut on authoring — the constitution forbids citing an internal path from a shipped file — and replaced with this public source. Do not reinstate the figure. |

Note this is the author's own ten-month revision of *Don't Build Multi-Agents* (already cited elsewhere in this file) and it **narrows** rather than reverses the original — cite the pair, not one half. |
| **Agents under-select structural replacement — measured.** On held-out checkpoints, two of three frontier models met rising requirements by making functions bigger *"rather than moving things around"*; one went from **4.6% to 16.8%** duplicated lines, inflecting *"roughly where new requirements start fighting the initial design"* | `references/repair-or-replace.md` → "rather than moving things around" | **PRIMARY** | Dex Horthy (HumanLayer), *Why Software Factories Fail: Benchmarking the new frontier* — https://x.com/dexhorthy/status/2081797628552270027 . Held-out black-box tests, 3 models × 3 problems × 17 checkpoints. **Limit recorded:** the newest model in the same run shows no duplication drift, so this may describe a defect that is closing — do not state it as permanent. |
| The **scoring shape** that would reward it: benchmark reward pairs the fix with `PASS_TO_PASS` — *"did you do it without breaking anything else?"* — and *"there is no penalty for eroding codebase maintainability"* | `references/repair-or-replace.md` → "there is no penalty for eroding codebase maintainability" | **PRIMARY (mechanism proposed, not demonstrated)** | Dex Horthy, *Why Software Factories Fail (parts 1 and 2)* — https://x.com/dexhorthy/status/2080697380379427275 . | **The bake-off's blind spot.** A pre-existing check covers only behaviour someone wrote down; what a rewrite loses is the behaviour nobody did | `references/repair-or-replace.md` → "the behaviour nobody did" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | Follows from **compound-v:extracting-specs**, which already owns recovering an undocumented contract. Recorded because the replace-move's only hard guard is the check, and a green bake-off over a thin suite is a confident way to ship a regression. No new source needed; the routing is the finding. |
| A project is **3–7 slices**; below that it is a feature, above that they are tasks | `get-shit-done` → "3–7 slices" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | Authoring knob in the same family as the `N=3` and 200-line caps. It encodes the tier table's boundary, not a claim about the world. No citation needed. |

Deliberately carries **no number** — see the existing "measured hit rate" row above, which records why (the supporting figure lives only in a private corpus). |

The skill cedes it by name in its opening — "It never asks whether the thing is *defensible* — moat, durability, whether the next model release deletes this is **compound-v:startup-taste**" — and substitutes a checkable proxy: reachable + survived + evolvable, which are verifiable at finish time where defensibility is not. ## Post-apply grounding pass (2026-08-18)

*Rows for claims the 2026-08 apply pass introduced into skill bodies with no entry anywhere above. Found
by reading the added lines of `git diff` for numbers, percentages, named products, named researchers and
quoted assertions, then checking each against a public source. Where a source could not be found the row
says so plainly instead of inventing one — a row that admits it is unsourced is worth more than a
confident one that guessed, and it is the only way the next reader can tell the two apart. Rows marked
**UNSOURCED** are open items for the skill's owner: attach a public URL or cut the sentence.*

### agent-security

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| A malicious PR **title** reached a coding agent's review path; the subprocess inherited `ANTHROPIC_API_KEY` and `GITHUB_TOKEN` and the agent posted them back as a PR comment, with GitHub itself as the exfiltration channel — rated Critical (CVSS 9.4) and bountied | `agent-security` → "rated Critical (CVSS 9.4) and bountied" | PRIMARY | Aonan Guan, Zhengyu Liu and Gavin Zhong (Johns Hopkins), "Comment and Control: Prompt Injection to Credential Theft in Claude Code, Gemini CLI, and GitHub Copilot Agent" — https://oddguan.com/blog/comment-and-control-prompt-injection-credential-theft-claude-code-gemini-cli-github-copilot/ ; press write-up — https://www.securityweek.com/claude-code-gemini-cli-github-copilot-agents-vulnerable-to-prompt-injection-via-comments/ . Anthropic rated it Critical (CVSS 9.3, later 9.4) and paid a bounty; **no CVE was assigned**, so cite the disclosure and never a CVE id. Closes UNGROUNDED P1 #21, which the GitHub-MCP row only ever covered by shape. The generalizing half — a subprocess inherits its parent's environment — is POSIX process semantics and needs no source. |
| A skill or command file can execute shell **at render time**, before the model sees the prompt — so writing one is code execution, not persuasion | `agent-security` → "A skill file can carry shell that executes at render time" | PRIMARY | Claude Code skills reference — https://code.claude.com/docs/en/skills (verbatim: "The `` !`<command>` `` syntax runs shell commands before the skill content is sent to Claude. The command output replaces the placeholder"). This is why the memory-poisoning carve-out must block *creation* of instruction files and not only modification. |
| Worktree/workspace isolation is a **development** boundary, not a security one — parallel agents still run as you, with your permissions, on your machine | `agent-security` → "workspace isolation is development isolation, not a security boundary" | PRIMARY on the mechanism; **UNSOURCED on the attribution** | `git worktree` is checkout management with no privilege boundary — https://git-scm.com/docs/git-worktree — and the sandbox-versus-approval split it is contrasted against is the already-cited OpenAI "Running Codex Safely" — https://openai.com/index/running-codex-safely/ . The skill says "the tools that run agents in parallel say so **in their own docs**"; that plural attribution is not pinned to any URL here. Either name one runner's doc line or drop the appeal to docs and keep the mechanism, which stands on its own. |
| Permission layers compose by **narrowing only** — a nested layer that would widen a lock set above it is dropped, never honoured | `agent-security` → "Composition may only narrow" | JUDGMENT-CALL / CANONICAL | Standard policy-composition semantics (deny-overrides, most-restrictive-wins), the same rule managed enterprise settings apply over user settings. The warrant is stated in the skill itself: anything that can write a lower layer could otherwise grant itself what you denied. No citation needed. |
| A path-based denial does not reach the shell — denying a read of `.env` does nothing about `cat .env`, so never allow-list a general-purpose interpreter | `agent-security` → "so every path-based denial must be paired with control of the shell" | JUDGMENT-CALL / CANONICAL | Follows from what a path matcher can and cannot see; allow-listing an interpreter is allow-listing everything it can run. Reasoning, not a measured claim. No citation needed. |

### council

*Rewritten 2026-08-22 after a four-agent red team broke the first draft. Three claims in that draft
were **false against this corpus** and are recorded here so they are not reintroduced: that
rank-aggregation has "no measurement"; that a maximally-blind reviewer is best; and that repeated
sampling of one model yields little diversity. The 4x/15x-token and ~80%-variance figures live in
the `context-engineering` row above and are not duplicated.*

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The generation-verification gap** — repeated sampling of ONE model yields abundant diversity (coverage vs. samples "follows an exponential power law"), but majority voting "plateaus after 10 or 50 samples" because voting selects without access to truth | `council` → "Generation is cheap; selection is the bottleneck" | PARTLY GROUNDED; local corpus | Stanford CS329A lecture on Large Language Monkeys / repeated sampling, local transcript corpus. **Supersedes** a first-draft claim that one model sampled repeatedly returns "N similar answers" — that was refuted: the same lecture reports models "already know a whole lot more than what you get out of them when you just ask them once." Pin the Large Language Monkeys paper before citing the power law as a measurement. |
| **Verifiability can be manufactured** — "it is possible to actually improve the asymmetry by front-loading some research about the task" | `council` → "Can you *manufacture* one?" | PRIMARY | Jason Wei, "Asymmetry of verification and verifier's law"; pin the public URL from the source listing when next touched. This is the rung the first draft omitted entirely. |
| A reviewer must be blind to the author's **reasoning trace** but **fed the goal and constraints** — diff-only vs. context-aware over 15 PRs measured **+17.5% precision, +43.3% recall, +25.4% F1** for the context-fed reviewer; and "Having the agent that wrote the code review its own code is insufficient" | `council` → "blind ≠ starved" | PARTLY GROUNDED; local corpus | DeepLearning.AI *AI Code Review* short course, local transcript corpus — the only controlled context ablation in the corpus. **Supersedes** the first draft's "Fresh context is the whole asset. Do not spend it.", which was refuted by this measurement. Corroborating mechanism (context rot, not only bias): the reviewer "re-discover[s] any context it needs as it reads the code from scratch" — Cognition, https://cognition.ai/blog/multi-agents-working . |
| Self-review shares evaluator/generator context, the in-context reward-hacking trigger; and a model re-reading its own draft repeats its own hallucination | `council` → "Fresh reviewers, not the authors" | PRIMARY | Lilian Weng, "Reward Hacking in RL" — https://lilianweng.github.io/posts/2024-11-28-reward-hacking/ ("Identical context between the evaluator and generator is crucial for ICRH"); and "Extrinsic Hallucinations in LLMs" — https://lilianweng.github.io/posts/2024-07-07-hallucination/ . |
| **Peer ranking IS shipped and IS measured** — pairwise debates aggregated into Elo, 78.4% top-1 on GPQA Diamond by highest Elo, working because "the law of large numbers smooths out noise into a meaningful global ordering after enough matches" | `council` → "not because nobody has made it work" | PARTLY GROUNDED; secondary | Google "Towards an AI co-scientist", via a private-corpus teardown; LangChain's shipped tournament pattern ("a **judge subagent** compares them in pairs") in the local transcript corpus. **This row exists to kill a false first-draft claim** that rank-aggregation had "no shipped precedent and no measurement". The surviving argument is match volume, not absence of evidence. Pin the co-scientist paper before quoting 78.4% externally. |
| Judge failure modes, in the order they bite a council: **length bias** ("tend to bias toward longer responses"), **order bias** (unfixable by instruction — the sentence saying order "does not affect your judgment" was tested and the bias persisted; direction is model-specific), **allow ties**, and **evidence before verdict** | `council` → "Length bias first" | PRIMARY | applied-llms.org — https://applied-llms.org/ (length, order-swap, ties) and Lilian Weng, "Reward Hacking in RL" — https://lilianweng.github.io/posts/2024-11-28-reward-hacking/ , citing Wang et al. https://arxiv.org/abs/2305.17926 . **Do not** attach a ">20% performance difference" to judge order bias — that figure measures transformer *retrieval* position bias and was welded to the wrong mechanism in the first draft, twice (body and table). |
| Prefer **decomposed binary sub-checks** to one graded scale; the LLM-specific scale failure is systematic **over**-scoring (a judge rated a deliberately bad deck "between 2.8 and 4.0"), fixed by anchored examples — the middle-clustering finding is about *human* annotators | `council` → "Decompose instead of scaling" | PRIMARY + local | Hamel Husain, "Evals FAQ" — https://hamel.dev/blog/posts/evals-faq/ ("sub-components with their own binary checks"); over-scoring measured in an Anthropic workshop, local transcript corpus. `compound-v:evals` owns judge design; council defers to it rather than re-deriving. |

### critical-thinking · recheck

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| On a **correctness-checkable** claim, unaided self-critique measurably degrades the result — the critique manufactures false positives and false negatives, and out of the box a model is no better at verifying than at generating; run the checker instead | `critical-thinking` → "Self-critique on checkable claims measurably" · `recheck` → "across formal reasoning domains" | PRIMARY | Jie Huang et al., "Large Language Models Cannot Self-Correct Reasoning Yet", ICLR 2024 — https://arxiv.org/abs/2310.01798 (intrinsic self-correction, with no external feedback, leaves performance flat or *degraded*; models talk themselves out of correct answers). Closes UNGROUNDED P1 #14, previously the most citable unsourced claim in the kit. Note the boundary both skills already respect: the finding indicts *unaided* self-critique on checkable claims, not review with an external signal — which is why `recheck` step 5 refuses a verdict without an executed check, and why `critical-thinking` runs its gates only where no formal checker exists. |

### designing-agents

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Cap the sub-agent tree at one level and enforce it in code — "every shipped harness that ships sub-agents caps nesting at depth 1; a worker cannot spawn a worker" | was anchored in `designing-agents` at the phrase "caps nesting at depth 1" | **WRONG AS WRITTEN — do not ship the universal** | Contradicted by the largest shipped counter-example. Claude Code's own sub-agents reference: "By default, a subagent can spawn subagents of its own, up to three layers below the main conversation", with `CLAUDE_CODE_MAX_SUBAGENT_SPAWN_DEPTH` configurable and **1 meaning nesting disabled** — https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents . So depth-1 is a *setting*, not the universal default, and "every shipped harness" is false. **What survives** is the design advice minus the universal quantifier: depth-1 is a defensible default, and the enforcement point is real and citable — the same page's recipe for a read-only reviewer is to omit `Agent` from its tool list or add it to `disallowedTools`, which is exactly the skill's "structural, not advisory" claim. Rewrite to "make it structural; several harnesses let you set the depth to 1, and a read-only reviewer should simply not carry the spawn tool." |
| Route between two strong models as a capability router; the cheap-primary escalation form does not pay because the primary sets the ceiling | `designing-agents` → "the cheap-primary form of this shipped and didn't pay" | **UNSOURCED** | "Shipped and didn't pay" is a claim about a real product with no product named and no source. The adjacent half — a worker cannot reliably tell when it doesn't know, so the trigger must be an observable external signal — is the kit's own reasoning and is fine as a judgment call. Name the system or drop "shipped". |

### simplest-thing-that-works

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Anti-underkill, measured: a repo-level code-translation validator **stripped back to a single well-prompted agent** loses a large share of its accuracy, worst on the largest codebases | `simplest-thing-that-works` → "lost a large share of its accuracy" | PRIMARY **— RESOLVED 2026-09-01: the unmatched number was removed** | MatchFixAgent, "Language-Agnostic Autonomous Repository-Level Code Translation Validation and Repair" — https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.16187 . The paper's ablation reports the standalone-agent baseline dropping validation accuracy by **42.3%** on the 1,091 non-disputed instances, and reaching only **47.7%** on the 88 disputed cases MatchFixAgent gets right. Neither is 58%, and no "~58% on average" appears. Either re-derive the figure from the paper's own table and quote *that*, or drop to "loses a large share of its accuracy". Do not ship 58% as a measurement. **Resolved:** the skill now reads "lost a large share of its accuracy" — the qualitative form this row prescribed. The argument never needed the decimal, which is the usual case when a number cannot be landed on its source. |
| Rungs compose: a deterministic pre-pass computing six semantic analyses in one call before any agent runs paid for itself on **both** accuracy and cost | `simplest-thing-that-works` → "six semantic analyses of source and target in a single one-shot call" | PRIMARY, with the two smaller deltas needing a pairing check | Same paper — https://arxiv.org/abs/2509.16187 . Removing the semantic-analysis pre-pass drops accuracy by **39.7%** (the skill's "~40%" is right) and costs **7.5%** more time (61.28h against 56.71h — the skill's "~7.5%" is right). The paper's other two deltas are **4.2%** and **6.2%**; the skill assigns ~4% to agent turns and ~6% to input tokens, which is the **opposite pairing** to the one this pass read off the source. Check the table and fix the pairing before shipping — the direction of the argument is unaffected either way, but two swapped numbers are exactly the defect the ledger is meant to catch. |

Grounds the adjacent wrong-stage warning too: escape-velocity tactics before the first network tips is the classic mis-sequencing. |
| Never let moat-forecasting decide whether to start — picking between two ideas by which has the bigger five-year moat is a named mistake | `startup-taste` → "that just isn't how it works" | **UNSOURCED (quoted)** | The sentence carries quotation marks, which asserts someone said it, and no one is named. The surrounding argument — a moat is defensive, so you need something worth defending before the question is well-posed — is the kit's own reasoning and would be fine as a stated stance. Either attribute the quote or remove the quotation marks. |
| Concentrate on one channel only **after** about three cheap parallel tests; do not claim most companies' first thousand users came through one door | `startup-taste` → "run about three cheap tests in parallel first" | PRIMARY (correction already applied) | This is the corrected form of `founder-distribution` claim 3 above: Bullseye deliberately runs about three channels in parallel at the test stage, and the "one channel" frequency claim has no counting behind it. The skill now says so in its own body and names the vendor-page provenance of the circulating figure — which is the ledger's do-not-cite entry, enforced in shipped text. No new citation needed. |

|---|---|---|---|
| The first cohort is a **selection** decision: product evolution is path-dependent on what the early adopters wanted, so recruiting whoever answers chooses blind which preferences get compiled in permanently | `founder-distribution` → "a first cohort that would pay $150,000 for an impractical car" | PRIMARY (same talk as the `get-shit-done` rows) | Y Combinator, *How To Get Your First Users* — https://youtu.be/0kARDVL2nZg . The path-dependence claim and the Tesla Roadster worked case are the same source already indexed in the `get-shit-done` section above (whose own anchor for it was retired on 2026-08-18 when that bullet was cut; the claim is unchanged and lives on here). **Caveat recorded honestly:** this pass did not re-pull the transcript, so the `$150,000` figure and the Model-Y-versus-Lamborghini comparison are carried on that attribution rather than re-verified. Check them against the transcript before treating either as a measurement. |
| Customers 1–3 come from the founder's first- and second-degree network almost without exception; customers 4–10 come from work that does not scale; prospecting tooling starts paying only once the count has bought a message worth scaling | `founder-distribution` → "there were basically no counter-examples to this pattern" · `founder-distribution` → "prospecting tooling starts paying somewhere around 10" | PRIMARY on attribution; **not re-verified** | Attributed in the skill to a YC founder survey and quoted directly; the plausible source is the same YC talk above. The quoted line and the 10–20 threshold were **not** confirmed against a transcript in this pass. This is the one place in the section where a direct quotation rests on attribution alone — pull the transcript and pin it, or unquote it. |

The surrounding Gmail/Superhuman contrast keeps its existing rows. |

### product-taste · finishing · writing-plans · verification-before-completion

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Nested corners are **concentric**: inner radius = parent radius − the padding between them | `product-taste` → "make nested corners concentric" | JUDGMENT-CALL / CANONICAL | Interface-craft arithmetic, in the same family as the `16px` and `0.96` constants already ruled canonical above; corroborated by Rauno Freiberg's interface guidelines — https://github.com/raunofreiberg/interfaces . It is a construction rule, not a claim about the world. No citation needed. |
| Worktree teardown order — merge, `cd` out, remove the worktree, then delete the branch — because the dangerous end is the one that returns success | `finishing` → "Worktree cleanup order" | PRIMARY / verifiable | `git worktree` reference — https://git-scm.com/docs/git-worktree : `git branch -d` refuses a branch checked out in another worktree, while `git worktree remove` has no cwd guard, so removing from inside strands the shell in a deleted directory. Both behaviours are reproducible in one command each; the quoted error strings are git's own. |
| Two independent caps on a plan: ~200 lines for the reader, and a per-task file count for the executor | `writing-plans` → "a task spanning nine or ten" | JUDGMENT-CALL | Authoring knobs, extending the existing `Cap the plan at 200 lines` P2 ruling to the second axis. The reasoning is stated inline (a task that reads nine or ten files spends its window reading before it writes, and batching groups tasks rather than splitting one). No citation needed. |
| Verify a **read-only** subagent through its evidence — open two or three of its quotes at the locators it gave — rather than redoing its work | `verification-before-completion` → "trust explorer results" | JUDGMENT-CALL on the rule; **UNSOURCED** on the attribution | The rule is sound and the failure it names is real and checkable (a citation pass only *places* references, so an unsupported claim ships uncited rather than flagged). But "shipped orchestrators tell the parent to trust explorer results" is an appeal to unnamed products. Name one or drop the appeal; the evidence-spot-check rule stands without it. |

### recheck · searching-patterns · test-driven-development · systematic-debugging · product-taste · context-engineering

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| The most common false rejection by a production verifier is **inventing a requirement the spec never carried** — missing edge cases, absent validation, extra robustness — and it is the top reason correct in-scope work never converges | `recheck` → "the one production verifiers name as their most common false rejection" | **UNSOURCED** | A specific empirical claim about production verifiers, attributed to no one, carrying a load-bearing carve-out (it is what licenses the reviewer to *stop* flagging). It is also directly adjacent to a well-grounded neighbour (the Codex-CLI don't-flag-intentional rows above), which is exactly the proximity trap this ledger warns about. Attach a source or restate it as the kit's own reviewing stance. The two carve-outs beside it need nothing: a vulnerability is grounded by the `agent-security` rows and a broken contract by the CodeRabbit contract-widening row. |
| The bar does not rise between rounds — a new objection counts only as a demonstrable defect or an already-stated unmet criterion | `recheck` → "Anti-ratchet" | JUDGMENT-CALL | The convergence half of the existing N=3 ruling, made explicit: a reviewer that finds a fresh reason each pass spends the cap on correct work and terminates in a false ARCHITECTURE_CONCERN. Same recipe-knob family as the signal-density cap. No citation needed. |
| The **installed copy** is the top rung of the lookup ladder — version-exact by construction, one grep, no network, and the only rung that survives a sandboxed run | `searching-patterns` → "The copy you actually installed" | JUDGMENT-CALL / CANONICAL | Follows from what a lockfile guarantees: the vendored source *is* the contract you are compiling against, so a docs-versus-lockfile mismatch is impossible there and possible everywhere else. Extends the existing local-convention-overrides row rather than making a new empirical claim. No citation needed. |
| "Simplest" has a floor: against one visible case the shortest passing code is that case's expected value, which is the reward hack aimed at the implementation instead of the test | `test-driven-development` → "simplest has a floor" | JUDGMENT-CALL (illustration) + PRIMARY by reuse | The arithmetic is self-evident and the worked example needs no source. That models reach it on their own under optimization pressure is already grounded twice above — Lilian Weng on proxy-reward exploitation, and the Cursor reward-hacking measurement in the `evals` row of this section. The check the skill prescribes (look for the assertion's literals sitting in the code path) is a recipe knob. |
| Reading the relevant code before hypothesizing can consume a large fraction of a session's tokens and still be the fastest path — the felt urgency is a bias to override, not a budget signal | `systematic-debugging` → "expect that to feel expensive" | JUDGMENT-CALL | Debugging discipline, stated as reasoning rather than measurement — no figure is claimed, which is why no source is needed. Note it is the same bias the late-session "suddenly everything is done" red flag names in `verification-before-completion`. |
| Prototype motion, keyboard navigation and touch in running code, not in a static design tool — the static tool shows keyframes, never the feel | `product-taste` → "in code, not Figma" | JUDGMENT-CALL / CANONICAL | Follows from what a static artboard can represent; the animation gate it feeds is already grounded by Emil Kowalski, "Great Animations" — https://emilkowal.ski/ui/great-animations . The naming of one commercial tool is illustrative, not a claim about that product. No citation needed. |
| Scope the context firewall by what the child **did**: a reader's digest compresses cleanly, an actor's implicit decisions die in a summary — so keep an acting child's trace reachable | `context-engineering` → "read the child's trajectory" | PRIMARY by reuse | Walden Yan, Cognition, "Don't Build Multi-Agents" — https://cognition.ai/blog/dont-build-multi-agents : actions carry implicit decisions, and its Principle 1 is to share full agent *traces* rather than just messages. Already cited above for the concurrent-writer divergence; this row records that the read-versus-act asymmetry is the same finding applied to the sub-agent boundary, so it needs no new source. |

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |

Not a claim about the world — a claim about one environment on one day. |
| A search engine's digest of a post is a **paraphrase, not the text** — usable to decide whether a source matters, never quotable | `references/prior-art.md` → "a paraphrase, not the text" | **JUDGMENT-CALL / verifiable** | Follows from what a summarising retrieval layer does. It is also the trap this pass hit while authoring: the digest read like a quotation and would have shipped as one. |

The budget table is the stage-1 confidence column reused as a spend rule; the DELETE-list-empty red flag is a heuristic about search framing, not a measurement. No citation needed. |

## references/completion-ledger.md (the 100% mechanism)

Added 2026-08-22 after a corpus sweep whose own critic found the pooled findings were "about grading
the WORK and zero about grading the GRADER" — which is why the integrity and fail-red rows below
exist at all. Most of this file is recipe knob; four rows carry real claims.

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| Declared scope becomes a machine-readable ledger of **per-function rows, all starting failing**, and the builder may change **only the status field** — Anthropic ran this at **over 200 rows** for one app | `get-shit-done` → "over 200 rows" | **PRIMARY** | Anthropic (Applied AI), *Effective harnesses for long-running agents* — https://www.anthropic.com/engineering/effective-harnesses-for-long-running-agents . The row grain is the load-bearing part: their list is per-feature ("a user can open a new chat, type in a query, press enter, and see an AI response"), not per-subsystem. A ledger at slice grain cannot see a missing function, which is the whole 90% failure. |
| **The agent must not be able to edit what grades it** — models trained on progressively gameable environments generalize to *directly rewriting their own reward function* | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "directly rewriting their own reward function" | **PRIMARY** | Denison et al. (Anthropic), *Sycophancy to Subterfuge: Investigating Reward-Tampering in Large Language Models* — https://arxiv.org/abs/2406.10162 . Abstract confirmed 2026-08-22: models "generalize zero-shot to directly rewriting their own reward function". Note the authors' own caveat — their operational definition admits false positives where the model edits the reward signal while honestly attempting the task — so cite it as a demonstrated failure mode, never as a claim about intent. |
| A generated checklist must not be marked complete by the thing that generated it, and a **requirement mapping to zero tasks blocks** | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "unmapped requirement as blocking" | **PRIMARY (with a stated limit)** | GitHub, *spec-kit* — https://github.com/github/spec-kit . Its `implement` step reads checklist state as a gate and its `analyze` step cross-checks requirements against tasks. **Honest limit, recorded so nobody over-reads it:** the implement gate STOPs and asks, and "yes" walks straight through — it is a soft gate. Our stronger form (a computed coverage command that exits non-zero) is the kit's own. |
| Stopping at 90% is **sometimes correct** — for a research portfolio, the last tenth of a mediocre line of work loses to the first tenth of a strong one | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "stopping at 90% of a mediocre line of work is often correct" | **PRIMARY (counter-evidence, deliberately included)** | Nicholas Carlini, *How to win a best paper award (doing research that matters)* — https://nicholas.carlini.com/writing/2026/how-to-win-a-best-paper-award.html , the "even if you're 90% of the way through a 'just ok' paper" passage. This is the honest counter-thesis to the whole skill and it is in the file for that reason. The boundary the skill draws: sound where scope is a **hypothesis**, wrong where scope is a **commitment**. A PRD is a commitment, so abandonment stays legal but only as an explicit `dropped` row with a name on it. |
| **A row's check must be seen red before it may go green** (a check that passes against an empty implementation measures nothing) | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "The check failed against nothing first" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | Self-evident by construction, and it is **compound-v:test-driven-development**'s red step applied to the ledger rather than a new claim. Deliberately carries no benchmark attribution: a draft cited specific RL-environment filters for the reference-passes/empty-fails pair, and the citation could not be verified from a public source, so it was cut and the rule left standing on its own logic. |
| The **scope delta** report (`declared N · discovered +D · dropped −X · passed · blocked · todo`) and append-always/flip-never | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "A run whose denominator only ever shrinks" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | Recipe knobs. The attempt cap this leans on is **compound-v:systematic-debugging**'s existing N=3 row — independently corroborated by a shipped harness that also requires three consecutive turns before a goal may be marked blocked. |
| **CUT on recheck 2026-08-22 — do not reinstate:** the commit-sha staleness rule, and "the agent that files a row may not close it" | — | **REMOVED** | The sha rule had no fixed point: the commit recording a row's evidence advances `HEAD` past every sha it just wrote, so the whole denominator is stale the instant it is written. Decay is real but already caught by the build loop's slice-open re-run, so the rule was redundant machinery on top of an existing check. The filer/closer separation was dead text — the pipeline is single-writer by construction and never supplied a second party; red-first plus the integrity rule plus **compound-v:recheck** already close self-certification. Both were flagged as the kit's own inventions when they shipped, which is what made them cheap to cut. |
| Structure has a real cost, and the honest split is that the ledger's **content** is endorsed while its **volume** is indicted: *"Add an instruction, example, or tool only when it fixes a measured failure mode"*, with *"process instructions for behavior the model already performs reliably"* on the trim list and *"success criteria and stopping conditions"* on the keep list | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "only when it fixes a measured failure mode" | **PRIMARY** | OpenAI, prompting guidance shipped inside the `openai/codex` repository — https://github.com/openai/codex (`codex-rs/skills/src/assets/samples/openai-docs/references/prompting-guide.md`). Read on disk 2026-08-22. This is the kit's anti-overfit law arriving from the strongest possible direction: the vendor of a shipped coding harness warning against exactly the density of process this file prescribes, while explicitly keeping the part it prescribes. Corroborated by a practitioner report that benchmarked spec-driven kits and found many make agents measurably worse — that measurement lives only in a private corpus, so it is carried here as context, not as a citation. |
| **Grain scales with the run**: a single-turn task gets no ledger — one shipped harness skips its planning tool for *"roughly the easiest 25%"* of tasks, keeps a short deliberately non-persisted checklist for the rest, and stores one durable objective per thread | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "roughly the easiest 25%" | **PRIMARY** | `openai/codex` — https://github.com/openai/codex (`codex-rs/core/gpt-5.2-codex_prompt.md`, and the `state/goals_migrations` schema for the one-objective-per-thread record). Verified on disk 2026-08-22. **Recorded as a genuine tension, not a resolved one:** codex's durable grain is one objective; Anthropic's long-running-harness work used 200+ per-feature rows for the multi-session build case, which is this skill's case. The row ledger is defended on scope, not on consensus. |
| The done rule and the anti-scope-shrink rule are **independently reinvented** by a shipped harness — *"Finish with all items completed or explicitly canceled/deferred before ending the turn"* and *"do not redefine success around a smaller or easier task"* | `get-shit-done` → "dropped`-with-attribution" | **PRIMARY** | `openai/codex` — https://github.com/openai/codex (`codex-rs/core/gpt_5_2_prompt.md`; `codex-rs/prompts/templates/goals/continuation.md`). Verified on disk 2026-08-22. The strongest corroboration the design has: arrived at independently by another lab, in production prompt text. |
| The integrity rule is best implemented as a **tool schema**, not prose — a shipped harness restricts the model's write to a status enum, pins the objective outside the model's reach, and refuses a new goal while one is unfinished | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "not a grader" | **PRIMARY** | `openai/codex` — https://github.com/openai/codex (`codex-rs/ext/goal/src/tool.rs`). Verified on disk 2026-08-22. Stronger than the kit's own framing, which states it as a rule the agent is asked to follow. Where a harness can express it in the type system, that is the correct form. |
| A human gate belongs at the **action boundary (irreversibility)**, not on row taste — a human placed on "does this feel right" becomes the bottleneck | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "select them by irreversibility, not by" | **JUDGMENT-CALL (corrected on recheck)** | The earlier shipped wording named taste, tone and usability as the human-flip rows. A transcript sweep found practitioners uniformly select by irreversibility of the action and warn that a review-everything human is the bottleneck; the supporting quotes live only in a private corpus, so the rule is carried as the kit's own and aligned to the axis **compound-v:make-it-stable** already defines. Recorded because it was a shipped error, not a refinement. |
| An **inverted diagnostic** closes part of the weak-check gap: every row green with a bad outcome means the row set is wrong, and that is the author's failure | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "the row set is wrong" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | The logic is self-evident once stated and needs no source; it was surfaced by a private-corpus sweep and is carried as a recipe knob. It converts a gap this file previously declared unfixable into a partly-handled one, which is why it earned its two sentences. |
| Recon must emit a **file:line map, not prose**, and the build order is gated by artifacts rather than by instruction | `get-shit-done-2-recon` → "Recon returns a file:line map, not prose" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | Falsifiability logic: a cited line either exists or it does not, so a hallucinated architecture is grep-detectable, where prose is not. Recorded honestly: the sweep found **no** source describing an *enforced* look-up-prior-art-before-planning gate — the only hard enforcement observed anywhere is a plan mode that withholds the write tools. Mandating the ordering is therefore this kit going beyond documented practice, which is why it is tied to an artifact rather than to a sentence. |

## The engine — hooks/stop-ledger, scripts/ledger.sh, scripts/ledger-extract.jq

Added 2026-08-22. The ledger withheld the verdict; nothing acted on it. These rows record what the
engine rests on and, more usefully, what an adversarial recheck made us cut.

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| A `Stop` hook can refuse a turn's exit, and the harness caps it at one block per turn via `stop_hook_active` | `get-shit-done` → "refuses the exit" | **PRIMARY / verifiable** | Claude Code hook reference — https://docs.claude.com/en/docs/claude-code/hooks . The cap is the harness's own convergence guard and is the only thing keeping this gate from wedging a session; the kit adds no second one on purpose (see the removed row below). |
| **An unreadable ledger resolves toward OPEN, never toward done** — an unknown status, a non-object row, a duplicate id, or buckets that do not sum are all treated as unfinished | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "closed set of exactly five words" | **JUDGMENT-CALL (from a reproduced defect)** | Not a claim about the world — a rule adopted after an adversarial recheck reproduced its opposite: one status typo (`done` for `passed`) made a row invisible to both the hook and the audit command, which printed 93% and exited 0 on unfinished work. Both halves now share `scripts/ledger-extract.jq` precisely so they cannot certify a run in disagreement. |
| **REMOVED, do not reinstate:** the stale-progress counter and its `.claude/.gsd-nudge` state file | — | **REMOVED** | Shipped for one commit as the anti-busywork mechanism, then cut on a recheck's recommendation. It was defeated by a `passed`→`todo` regression (resolved count going *down* read as progress), resettable with an invented `blocked`, shared across concurrent sessions, unable to advance on a read-only `.claude/`, and — the reason it had to go rather than be fixed — it disarmed the gate after three quiet turns, which is the signature of a hard tail rather than of busywork. It made the gate weakest exactly where it was needed. The anti-busywork property lives in the block message being bound to rows, not in a counter. |
| A container object is not a row, even when it carries its own `status` | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "closed set of exactly five words" | **JUDGMENT-CALL (regression record)** | A recursive extractor counted slices as rows and read an 18-row ledger as 23. Fixed by excluding objects that hold a `rows` array; pinned by a regression case in `scripts/hooks-test.sh` that was confirmed red before it was confirmed green. Recorded because the shape that broke it is the documented shape. |
| The audit command **computes** completion and exits non-zero while anything is open; it never asserts a number | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "Compute coverage; never assert it" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | Recipe knob. The three exit codes (0 done / 1 open / 2 cannot tell) exist so that "cannot tell" can never be mistaken for "done", which is the failure the row above records. |

## Stage 5 — Discharge (the post-merge phase)

Added 2026-08-22 after a three-lane sweep of the corpus and live web. The most valuable output of
that sweep was its **refusal list**, which is recorded here as a first-class row so the rejected
phases are not re-proposed later as improvements.

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| **The constraint moved past the merge.** Median feature-branch throughput rose while main-branch throughput fell, and time-to-green climbed | `references/mep-gate.md` → "Discharge before the landing commit" | **PRIMARY** | CircleCI, *Five takeaways from the 2026 Software Delivery Report* — https://circleci.com/blog/five-takeaways-2026-software-delivery-report/ . Verified by direct fetch 2026-08-22: *"For the median team, feature branch throughput increased 15%, but on the main branch, throughput fell by 7%"* and *"Recovery times are climbing too: 72 minutes to get back to green for the typical team, up 13% from last year."* The skill states the direction, not the digits, because a vendor's annual figure ages fast. |
| **Rollback is the first response, and it is what makes AI adoption pay.** The per-slice `irreversible` array that carried this was **CUT** on field evidence — 0 of 4 slices filled it and no code read it — and the reversible/irreversible axis stays where it already lives, in **compound-v:make-it-stable**. The claim is kept here because it is true and someone will propose the field again | — | **PRIMARY (mechanism removed)** | DORA — https://dora.dev/capabilities/version-control/ . Verified by direct fetch 2026-08-22: *"Frequent use of rollbacks amplifies the positive influence of AI on team performance."* This is why the `irreversible` array gates *arming* rollback rather than gating the deploy: an empty surface means revert is a real strategy. |
| **The red step needs a mutation form when the agent wrote the check** — run it against the real implementation and against a no-op stub, and require pass-then-fail | `references/completion-ledger.md` → "use the mutation form" | **PRIMARY (attribution unverified — treat the rule as the kit's own)** | Surfaced from SWE Atlas, https://arxiv.org/abs/2605.08366 , whose reported dominant failure of agent-written tests is assertions trivially true on a stubbed implementation. **The paper was not opened directly in this pass**, so the rule ships on its own logic (a check that passes both ways measures nothing) and the citation is a lead, not evidence. Open it before quoting a number from it. |
| **Discharge, do not merely delete**: a row's `status` is run-scoped and decays to a lie, while its `steps` and `evidence` are a regression contract the run already paid to write | `references/mep-gate.md` → "Discharge before the landing commit" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | The two research lanes split — one said keep the ledger, one said deleting is correct — and both were half right. The synthesis is the split by half-life, enforced by `bash scripts/ledger.sh --discharge`. Recipe knob; no external source claims it. |
| **An incident opens a row before it opens a fix** (`from: "incident"`), re-entering at Carve rather than in a maintenance lane | `get-shit-done-4-reckon` → "before it opens a fix" | **JUDGMENT-CALL** | Follows from the denominator rule: a second-class path is where the missing 10% relocates, because rows declared nowhere are rows the gate cannot see. |
| **REFUSED PHASES — considered against evidence and rejected. Do not re-propose as improvements.** | was anchored in `get-shit-done` at "What this stage refuses to become"; that paragraph was CUT with Stage 5 on 2026-08-22 (zero field adoption). **The refusals themselves stand — this row is now their only record, which is the point of writing them down.** | **REMOVED / DECLINED** | (a) A *monitoring/observability phase* — a noun with no obeyable sentence; the checkable version is a field on a unit of work, which is what `discharge.observable` is. (b) A *maintenance phase* — a second-class lane is where undeclared work hides. (c) A *lifecycle document* — the corpus verdict on the monolithic manual is that it rots instantly into a graveyard of stale rules. (d) A *design-doc stage* — the Claude Code team moved the other way, discussion happening in PRs and prototypes now that coding is not the bottleneck. (e) A *human-approval gate between recheck and release* — Spotify's own account is that having the owning team in the loop on every merged PR is what let them stay "a bit sloppy on test automation". (f) A *staging/soak gate* — Honeycomb did every responsible thing including staging and dogfood and still broke in production. (g) A *lights-off auto-merge tier* — the one practitioner who ran it in a maintained codebase concluded months later it would be easier to rewrite from scratch. (h) A *change-approval board* — refused on the Spotify evidence above; a draft of this row also cited DORA for it, and **that attribution was checked and did not hold** (the claim is not on the cited page), so it is dropped rather than carried. (i) *Fleet/estate mutation* and *scheduled entropy refactors* — real for a 27-service estate but a different skill's territory; naming them here would bloat the spine. (j) *Environment/credential blast-radius provisioning* — real and serious, and it belongs to **compound-v:agent-security**, which already owns it. |

## Upstream — the project this skill was ported from

`get-shit-done` began as a port of **gsd-core** (https://github.com/open-gsd/gsd-core). That upstream
was not read during the 2026-08-22 rewrite — a real prior-art miss on the kit's own Stage 2 rule, and
it was closed afterwards. The comparison is recorded because it is mostly *corroboration*, and
because the apparatus it refuses is as important as the one mechanism it adopts.

| Claim (short) | Anchor | Category | Source / note |
|---|---|---|---|
| The five-phase loop **Discuss → Plan → Execute → Verify → Ship** maps onto this skill's Carve → Recon → Build → Reckon → Discharge with no phase missing on either side | `get-shit-done` → "four stages" | **PRIMARY** | gsd-core README — https://github.com/open-gsd/gsd-core . Read on disk 2026-08-22. Useful mainly as a negative result: the port did not drop a phase, so the post-merge gap this kit closed in Stage 5 is genuinely downstream of both. |
| **Independent convergence on the discharge pass**: upstream's `nyquist` capability is *"Validation coverage audit that maps executed work back to tests and manual-only evidence"* | `references/mep-gate.md` → "Discharge before the landing commit" | **PRIMARY (corroboration)** | gsd-core `capabilities/nyquist/capability.json`. Arrived at independently here; the three-form discharge target (re-runnable command / production observable / named manual owner) is the same split upstream makes between tests and manual-only evidence. |
| **Independent convergence on the denominator cross-check**: upstream's `gap-analysis` cross-references every requirement id against the plans and reports coverage | `get-shit-done` → "scope nobody was assigned" | **PRIMARY (corroboration)** | gsd-core `capabilities/gap-analysis/capability.json`. This is now the third independent system converging on it — upstream, GitHub spec-kit's `analyze`, and this kit — which is the strongest evidence any rule here has. |
| **ADOPTED — the marker scan.** Stubs, TODOs, skipped tests and swallowed errors the implementation *left behind* are invisible to a denominator built from declared functions, because nobody declared them | `get-shit-done-3-build` → "You scanned the slice's diff for what you left behind" | **PRIMARY (adopted)** | gsd-core `capabilities/broken-windows/capability.json`: *"Cross-phase defect register accumulating stubs, TODOs, skipped tests, unrun verifies, and unmet truths… blocks /gsd-ship while any window is open unless explicitly waived with a recorded reason."* Adopted as a rule feeding the existing ledger rather than as a second artifact — the ledger already is that register, and a `WINDOWS.md` sibling would violate the one-state-file rule in **compound-v:handoff**. |
| **REFUSED — the apparatus.** 34 agents, 45 capabilities, 29 hooks, an npx installer and cross-runtime adapters | was anchored at the same cut paragraph; this row is now the record | **DECLINED** | Most of the 45 capabilities are runtime adapters (cursor, copilot, ollama, windsurf, codex…) irrelevant to a Claude Code skill kit, and the rest are a framework where this is a skill set. Also refused after reading: `assumption-delta` (a narrow, self-described "rarely-firing advisory" drift detector), `schema-gate` and `drift` (ORM-specific; the `irreversible` array covers the class), and `refactor-trigger` (estate hygiene, already refused in the Stage 5 row above). |
