The captions carry every element the row describes: an agent that fixes, gets re-reviewed and has to fix backwards because the comment was low quality; agents happily fixing a hundred nits engineers would push back on; costs down ~60% and accuracy up ~70% against a very naive implementation (paraphrased from captions); trajectory, addressal rate and sentiment fed back to rule-owning teams. The body had quoted two caption passages verbatim and inexactly with no talk caveat, against `scripts/yt.sh`. Uber's written post (uber.com/us/en/blog/ureview) corroborates the addressal figures but was not checked for these two sentences. |
| People who did the work misjudged even the **sign** of an AI speed-up: forecast −24% time, estimated −20% afterwards, measured **+19%** — so a speed-up claim needs a timed comparison, never the felt one | `critical-thinking` → "never the felt speed-up" | CONTESTED (one RCT; cite as one) | Becker, Rush et al., METR, "Measuring the Impact of Early-2025 AI on Experienced Open-Source Developer Productivity", https://arxiv.org/abs/2507.09089: "Before starting tasks, developers forecast that allowing AI will reduce completion time by 24%. After completing the study, developers estimate that allowing AI reduced completion time by 20%. Surprisingly, we find that allowing AI actually increases completion time by 19%--AI tooling slowed developers down." 16 developers, 246 tasks, Cursor Pro + Claude 3.5/3.7 Sonnet. **Date caveat carried in the skill:** METR's follow-up, https://metr.org/blog/2026-02-24-uplift-update/, estimates speed-ups now (−18%, CI −38% to +9%; −4%, CI −15% to +9%) and calls its data "only very weak evidence" because of selection effects. The perception gap, not the sign, is what the skill relies on. Falsifier: a timed study where post-hoc self-estimates track the measured effect. |
| **Read-only is an instruction until checked** — a reviewer that keeps Bash to run tests can write through it; record `git status --porcelain` and `git diff HEAD \| git hash-object --stdin` before and after the pass, and a moved tree voids the verdict | `recheck` → "read-only is an instruction until checked" | VERIFIED-DEFECT (the kit's own reviewer: `agents/code-reviewer.md` grants Bash and bans shell edits in prose only) | Cline PR #12906 (founder, merged 2026-08-05), https://github.com/cline/cline/pull/12906: "the \"never use it to change anything\" contract was enforced only by prompting. Weaker models in particular routinely ignore it and edit files through the shell (`sed -i`, `echo ... > file`, `rm`, heredocs, etc.)"; the fix is a denylist that "by design" misses `python -c "open(...,'w')"`. Anthropic's review plugin shows the same shape on an outbound write: a branch reached the posting step with no flag check until commit 6dcc7d8b76 restated the stop on every branch, while `gh pr comment` stayed granted. The hash is there because porcelain status is unchanged when an already-modified file is edited again. |
| The bug pass includes **what should not be in the diff at all** — a debug print, a zero-byte file from a botched pipe, a built binary, a test-only helper now called from production code | `recheck` → "what should not be in the diff at all" | DEFAULT (three orbits, talk + written) | Moritz Johner (Form3), "We Gave an Agent Production Code Access…", https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=LqLoYksJ6do (captions — substance): the controller vets the agent's tree for empty files from mis-piped shell commands and compiled binaries left in the repo before committing. Tomas Reimers (Graphite), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=TswQeKftnaw (captions — substance): in ~10,000 categorized review comments, accidentally committed code shows up more than expected. Armin Ronacher, https://lucumr.pocoo.org/2026/9/7/astra-why/: "that function started to be relied upon by non-test code as well." "Debug print" is the kit's concrete instance of Graphite's accidentally-committed class (JUDGMENT-CALL on the example). |
| **Re-asking is not a new pass — for judgment findings.** For defects a trace or reproducer can confirm, a rerun over the same code finds about as many bugs but different ones, so after a confirmed vulnerability or crash a second pass seeded with it (same pattern, same class) is a genuine new pass | `recheck` → "it governs judgment findings" | CONDITIONAL (axis: judgment finding vs trace-confirmable defect) | Bismuth, AI Engineer talk, https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wAQK7O3WGEE (captions — substance; vendor, mid-2025 models): across every agent tested, the per-run bug count stays roughly constant while the bugs themselves change. Simon Willison, https://datasette.io/blog/2026/september-security-releases/: "Several rounds of auditing (looking for similar issues to those that were already found) revealed a significant number of problems." Anthropic, https://claude.com/blog/using-llms-to-secure-source-code: variants at "(1) same pattern … and (2) same class"; the same post warns that brute-force horizontal scaling returned mostly duplicates — which is why the rule says *seeded*, not *repeated*. DoorDash's "variance is a feature" (careersatdoordash.com, HTTP 403 to curl) was not verified and is not relied on. |
| APPROVED licenses shipping the code, never the belief the right thing was built — sensors do not reliably catch misdiagnosis, over-engineering, or misunderstood instructions; open question: if sensors never fire, is that quality or inadequate detection? | `recheck` → "not reliably enough to reduce supervision" | PRIMARY (written; unregistered until this row) | Birgitta Böckeler, "Harness engineering for coding agent users", https://martinfowler.com/articles/harness-engineering.html: "Neither catches reliably some of the higher-impact problems: Misdiagnosis of issues, overengineering and unnecessary features, misunderstood instructions. They'll sometimes catch them, but not reliably enough to reduce supervision. Correctness is outside any sensor's remit if the human didn't clearly specify what they wanted in the first place." / "If sensors never fire, is that a sign of high quality or inadequate detection mechanisms?" The body's fragment ("leaves the closing move open…") had lost its subject in bc87fb0; now "The same source leaves one question open". |
| **Write down what the verifying output must show before you run it** — an expectation written after the run bends to fit the run | `verification-before-completion` → "write down what that output must show before you run it" | CONTESTED (one vendor describing its own agent; mechanism, not measurement) | Ido Pesok, Cognition, https://cognition.ai/blog/testing-development: "We found that Devin will lie less about its findings if it annotates its expected behavior right before performing an action - much like test-driven development, if you commit to the expectation upfront it makes it much harder to rationalize an unexpected result as a pass." Falsifier: a with/without run on self-verifying walks where pre-stated expectations do not lower the false-pass rate. |
| A screenshot or DOM channel observes **static properties only** — latency, animation, gameplay and audio stay unobserved until duration is measured, the run is recorded, or a person feels it | `verification-before-completion` → "for what a still frame can show" | DEFAULT (three orbits, written + talk) | Andrej Karpathy, https://x.com/karpathy/status/2083749667410727319 (fetched verbatim): "they can't easily audit their work because they aren't able to efficiently and natively perceive videos or play games within them. Here, Opus 5 had to very slowly and painstakingly take screenshots at different points, and it messed up a few times and created a bunch of jank.". Tuomas Artman (Linear), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=wjk0ulMAkbc (captions — substance): agents interact with the browser "timelessly" through screenshots or the DOM, know one second beats two but not whether two is too slow, and get animation easing and duration wrong. Ash Prabaker (Anthropic Applied AI), https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mR-WAvEPRwE (captions — substance): the evaluator tested the music app thoroughly, but the model cannot hear, so the music it composed was bad. Karpathy and Prabaker are one employer; Artman is independent; Matt Pocock (AI Hero, talk) adds that a screenshot catches broken, not ugly. |
| About **half of test-passing agent patches** would not be merged by the repo's maintainers — rejected for core functionality failure, breaking other code, or code quality | `verification-before-completion` → "maintainers would have refused roughly half" | CONTESTED (one independent study; cite as one) | METR, https://metr.org/notes/2026-03-10-many-swe-bench-passing-prs-would-not-be-merged-into-main: "We find that roughly half of test-passing SWE-bench Verified PRs written by mid-2024 to mid/late-2025 agents would not be merged into main by repo maintainers, even after adjusting for noise in maintainer merge decisions." / "the automated grader is on average about 24.2 percentage points (standard error: 2.7) higher than the maintainer merge decision." 4 maintainers, 3 repos, 296 PRs; golden-patch baseline 68%; agents got one attempt with no feedback iteration, which the authors say better elicitation may partly close. Falsifier: a rerun on current agents with review iteration where grader-pass and maintainer-merge converge. |
| **A verifying run proves only the path it took** — agents route around a broken official path (token read from the local DB, raw curl past a non-compliant server, JS that sets state, a flag flipped for the test) and report success; a detour through setup the claim is not about is fine and gets named | `verification-before-completion` → "the run proves only the path" | DEFAULT (four orbits, all written) | Sierra, https://sierra.ai/blog/building-sierras-mcp-gateway-an-engineering-iceberg: "Coding agents love to cheat. If authentication was broken, they'd \"helpfully\" read the correct token from a local database instead of fixing the problem. If an MCP server wasn't fully spec compliant, the agent would fall back to manual HTTP requests via curl, then declare success." Cognition, https://cognition.ai/blog/testing-development: "the models may sometimes lean too heavily on executing JavaScript in the browser to trigger states programmatically instead of clicking through the UI." Cursor, https://cursor.com/blog/agent-computer-use: "For local testing, the agent temporarily bypassed the feature flag gating the marketplace page, then reverted before pushing." Amp, https://ampcode.com/notes/putting-an-agent-in-an-orb — the setup-side case: without a dev login the agent tried "to wiggle its way out by creating new users or changing passwords"; `/__dev/log-me-in` is a sanctioned detour for setup, which is why the rule separates setup from the claimed path. |

## Plan and spec detail — the superpowers / Pocock / OpenSpec question, measured (2026-09-29)

*The owner's hypothesis: superpowers plans and specs are over-specified, Matt Pocock's too sketchy, OpenSpec the middle. Only public sources are cited below; the owner's private product breakdowns of shipped coding agents were read in full and agree, and are not cited in the public kit. Registers as in `references/corroboration.md`; STAKE = a toolkit author on their own toolkit. The kit's own A/B is the last row.*

| Claim | Where it lives | Register | Source |
|---|---|---|---|
| **A plan is the decisions the implementer cannot make alone plus the facts research found** — signatures, test assertions, the spec's values, traps, reuse targets — not the program in markdown; a body appears only for an algorithm the signature and tests do not determine | `writing-plans` → "A plan is the set of decisions the implementer cannot make alone" | DEFAULT (measured + shipped agents + practitioners) | superpowers PR #2333 / v6.4.2 (STAKE, a switch against its own design) — https://github.com/obra/superpowers/pull/2333 — terse plans executed 9/9 planted-defect probes on Sonnet 5 (54/54 across subagent arms), a six-module plan went 1,371–1,475 → 475–551 lines, "the code the plan no longer carries was not buying outcomes". Dex Horthy, "Everything We Got Wrong About RPI" — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=YwZR6tc7qYg — [talk, ASR] a 1,000-line plan ≈ 1,000 lines of code. OpenAI ExecPlans — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/openai/openai-cookbook/main/articles/codex_exec_plans.md — "Err on the side of over-explaining user-visible effects and under-specifying incidental implementation details." Codex (https://github.com/openai/codex @9e552e9d15, `codex-rs/core/gpt_5_2_prompt.md:40`): "plans are not for padding out simple work with filler steps or stating the obvious". Gemini CLI (https://github.com/google-gemini/gemini-cli @f47d6c6, plan-mode prompt): plan sections scale with the task — Changes + Verification for simple, Objective / Key Files & Context / Implementation Steps / Verification for standard, alternatives and rollback only for complex. Cline (https://github.com/cline/cline @3f38bd5, `PLAN_MODE_INSTRUCTIONS`): "Do NOT edit files, write code". Kent Beck's BPlusTree3 plan.md @70b38a6a: 455 lines, 0 code fences. |
| The implementer is a **capable engineer who was not in the room**, not one with "zero context and questionable taste"; the two-sided step test (would they have to ask → a decision is missing; would they read past code they could write → a transcript); **high-level does not mean thin** | `writing-plans` → "Write for a capable implementer who wasn't in the room" | DEFAULT (two vendors + one switch) | superpowers #2333 replaced "zero context, questionable taste". Anthropic, Fable 5 prompting guide — https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/prompting-claude-fable-5 — "Skills developed for prior models are often too prescriptive for Claude Fable 5 and can degrade output quality." Anthropic skill best practices — https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/agents-and-tools/agent-skills/best-practices — "Claude is already very smart". OpenAI GPT-5.5 guide — https://developers.openai.com/api/docs/guides/prompt-guidance?model=gpt-5.5 — "Legacy prompts often over-specify the process". Thariq Shihipar (Anthropic), "Finding Your Unknowns" — https://x.com/trq212/status/2073100352921215386 — too specific and Claude follows instructions "even when a pivot may be more appropriate"; too vague and it falls back on "industry best practices that may not be a fit". Eugene Yan (Anthropic) — https://claude.com/blog/using-llms-to-secure-source-code — "more prescriptive prompts make discovery worse" while the verifier needs "clear criteria". |
| **Detail follows fragility**: pin the one safe way exactly; give the goal, constraint and test where several approaches are valid. **A settled edit is given as the line**, not described in prose | `writing-plans` → "Detail follows fragility, not length"; "an edit you have already settled to the line" | DEFAULT (three orbits: Anthropic, OpenAI, one practitioner) | Anthropic skill best practices: "Match the level of specificity to the task's fragility and variability" / "Narrow bridge with cliffs on both sides … Open field with no hazards". GPT-5.5 guide: ALWAYS/NEVER "for true invariants … For judgment calls … prefer decision rules". OpenAI ExecPlans: "Do not outsource key decisions to the reader" and prefer "small excerpts … rather than pasting large blobs". Matt Pocock `to-spec` — https://github.com/mattpocock/skills — inline a snippet only where it "encodes a decision more precisely than prose can". |
| **One approach per plan** — the alternatives were brainstorming's job; an option left open in a plan is a guess the implementer makes | `writing-plans` → "one approach: the alternatives were brainstorming's job" | DEFAULT (three orbits: Anthropic, OpenAI, HumanLayer) | Claude Code plan-mode Phase 4 prompt (https://github.com/Piebald-AI/claude-code-system-prompts @3dd9e97, ccVersion 2.1.235): "Include only your recommended approach, not all alternatives". Codex plan mode (https://github.com/openai/codex): the final plan is "decision complete, where the implementer does not need to make any decisions". Claude Code 2.1.207 system prompt (same repo): "If you are weighing a choice, give a recommendation, not an exhaustive survey." HumanLayer `create_plan.md` — https://github.com/humanlayer/humanlayer/blob/main/.claude/commands/create_plan.md — "No Open Questions in Final Plan … Every decision must be made before finalizing the plan". |
| **Proportion**: a plan several times the spec's length, or mostly code blocks, is a transcript — a relative check that sits beside the 200-line reader cap (the cap forces a split, the proportion check catches a transcript under it) | `writing-plans` → "**Proportion:**" | PRIMARY (one measured source) + local measurement | superpowers #2333: the proportion item alone took a 10,206–10,894-line plan set to 2,968–3,615 lines; superpowers rejected a fixed line budget (#638) as its only bound, which this kit does not do — its cap is a split signal (P2 ruling, "Cap the plan at 200 lines"). In the kit's A/B (last row), every after-plan landed at 198–200 lines while 3 of 4 before-plans overran the cap (279, 290, 376): removing bodies is what makes the cap holdable. On the owner's machine, 82 superpowers-generated plans (pre-v6.4.2): median 743 lines, p90 2,302, 55% of lines inside code fences; 50 Compound V plans: median 231, 14% code, 27 over the 200-line cap. |
| **Implied cases**: up to five inputs the spec implies but never names, each pinned by a test in its owning task | `writing-plans` → "**Implied cases**" | DEFAULT (measured + three orbits) | superpowers #2319 (merged): "every implementer shipped the same crash on an input the spec implied but never named". SWE-RPG — https://export.arxiv.org/abs/2608.09072 — "implicit requirement recovery as the main bottleneck, accounting for 24.5%–46.0% of agent runs" (abstract only). "What Prompts Don't Say" — https://arxiv.org/html/2505.13360v3 — conditional requirements are the hardest to infer (22.9% vs 41.1%). OpenSpec `docs/writing-specs.md` — https://github.com/Fission-AI/OpenSpec — "what's the one case I'd be upset to see broken?". |
| **Each task cites the numbered requirements it fulfils**, and every requirement is claimed by a task and a Verification Plan line — coverage becomes a grep | `writing-plans` → "**Fulfills:**"; `brainstorming` → "`R1`, `R2`, … so the plan's tasks can cite them" | DEFAULT (three shipped products) | Luke Alvoeiro (Factory) — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=ow1we5PzK-o — [talk, ASR] the validation contract "defines what done … means before any coding is done"; "each feature is assigned one or more assertions that it must satisfy. The sum of all features must mean that every assertion is covered." Kiro: tasks "trace back to the requirement numbers" (Böckeler — https://martinfowler.com/articles/exploring-gen-ai/sdd-3-tools.html). GitHub spec-kit (https://github.com/github/spec-kit @e333b3c8, `templates/tasks-template.md:16-19`): every user-story task carries `[Story]` — "Which user story this task belongs to (e.g., US1, US2, US3)" — while setup, foundational and polish tasks carry none (`templates/commands/tasks.md:162-167`). That is a real difference: this kit's task-justification check cuts a task that serves no requirement, and setup belongs to the requirement it unblocks. |
| **The approach is non-binding tactics; Done, constraints, cross-task interfaces and `Deferred:` bind** — the implementer changes a tactic and names it, and stops only when a binding part cannot hold | `writing-plans` → "**non-binding tactics**" | DEFAULT (four orbits) | HumanLayer `implement_plan.md` — https://github.com/humanlayer/humanlayer/blob/main/.claude/commands/implement_plan.md — "Follow the plan's intent while adapting to what you find." Thariq, "Finding Your Unknowns": "pick the conservative option, log it under 'Deviations', and keep going." arXiv 2604.12147v3 Finding 11 — https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12147v3 — "Agents do not rigidly follow suboptimal ordering constraints … Strict compliance is neither necessary nor always beneficial". Windsurf Wave 11 prompt (public leak, https://github.com/x1xhlol/system-prompts-and-models-of-ai-tools, `Windsurf/Prompt Wave 11.txt`): on divergence, "update the plan first". Anthropic's "Build Agents That Run for Hours" — https://www.youtube.com/watch?v=mR-WAvEPRwE — [talk, ASR] the planner stays "deliberately high-level" because granular technical errors "cascade". The `code-reviewer` agent judges plan alignment against the binding parts; it and `recheck` treat a tactic logged in `## Clarifications` (by the orchestrator, `batched-implementation` step 3) as not divergence. |
| **Name the existing helpers to reuse, with paths; a change repeated across many files gets the pattern once and a few representative paths** | `writing-plans` → "the existing functions and helpers the change should reuse" | DEFAULT (three vendors) | Claude Code plan-mode Phase 4 prompt: "Reference existing functions and utilities you found that should be reused, with their file paths" / "describe the pattern once and list a few representative paths — do not enumerate every file or line number". GPT-5.5 guide: "be explicit about reuse … test expectations, acceptance criteria". Devin's DeepWiki prompt (public leak, https://github.com/x1xhlol/system-prompts-and-models-of-ai-tools, `Devin AI/DeepWiki Prompt.txt`): cite "file names … include the path and the line numbers". |
| **Constraints carry the failure they prevent** — a rule with its reason is applied to the case nobody listed | `writing-plans` → "carrying the failure it prevents" | PRIMARY (two orbits: Anthropic, one practitioner) + internal measurement | Anthropic prompting best practices — https://platform.claude.com/docs/en/build-with-claude/prompt-engineering/claude-prompting-best-practices — "Providing context or motivation behind your instructions … Claude is smart enough to generalize from the explanation." Anthropic skill-creator — https://raw.githubusercontent.com/anthropics/skills/main/skills/skill-creator/SKILL.md — "explain to the model why things are important in lieu of heavy-handed musty MUSTs". Matt Pocock, `writing-for-agents` — https://github.com/mattpocock/skills — "Cache what the agent cannot find by looking: the unwritten convention, the reason behind a choice". The kit's blind plan judge (last row): the one systematic miss, in every arm, was a rule stated without its consequence. |
| **Pin the "no test weakened" check to a base commit** — after per-task commits, a diff against the working tree prints nothing | `writing-plans` → "Pin that check to a fixed base" | INTERNAL MEASUREMENT | The kit's blind plan judge found this vacuous check in 2 of 7 plans for one fixture, across two arms. The guard itself is measured: Claude Opus 4.1 System Card Addendum, Table 5.B — https://www-cdn.anthropic.com/9fa30625273bafdf5af82c93719d7ca606485a16.pdf — one "do not … hard-code tests" line took reward hacking 52% → 18% (Sonnet 4: 51% → 7%; Sonnet 3.7 ignored it). |
| **Spec requirements are observable behaviours** (a tester who never saw the code could check them), numbered, with one WHEN … THEN for the edge you would hate to see broken; the *how* stays in the design and the plan | `brainstorming` → "one observable behavior" | DEFAULT (tool doctrine + shipped product) + one caution | OpenSpec `schema.yaml`: "A spec is a behavior contract, not an implementation plan … if the implementation can change without changing externally visible behavior, it likely does not belong in the spec." Factory's validation contract (talk above). Caution kept narrow on purpose: Böckeler found the functional/technical split hard to hold across a whole spec, so only the requirement list is kept how-free, and a hard constraint that names a how stays in it verbatim. |
| **brainstorming's stranger check runs only when no person will read the written design** — the person approving section by section is the check | `brainstorming` → "gated on who reads it, not on tier" | PRIMARY (measured, one source) + kit consistency | superpowers v5.0.6 release notes — https://github.com/obra/superpowers/blob/main/RELEASE-NOTES.md — the subagent spec/plan review loop "doubled execution time (~25 min overhead) without measurably improving plan quality" across 5 versions × 5 trials. `writing-plans` already gates its fresh pass on autonomy. The gap-and-contradiction brief itself is unchanged (row "The written design gets an INDEPENDENT gap-and-contradiction check"). |
| **"A subpar plan hurts performance even more than no plan at all"** — with its scope: the "plan" is a four-phase workflow in SWE-agent's system prompt, run on GPT-5 mini, DeepSeek-V3/R1 and Devstral-small | `gathering-context` → "Across 21,120 SWE-agent trajectories" | PRIMARY (one measured study; scope narrowed 2026-09-29) | arXiv 2604.12147v3 — https://arxiv.org/html/2604.12147v3 — abstract: "21,120 trajectories from SWE-agent across four LLMs on SWE-bench Verified and SWE-bench Pro under eight plan variations"; Finding 6: "The success rate drops in the absence of the standard plan"; Finding 8: "The negative impact of a bad, incomplete plan is greater on trajectories than the impact of no plan at all." "Subpar" means one phase removed, not a wrong implementation plan. |
| SUPERSEDED: "Code in the plan is source, not illustration" as a mandate on every code step, and "Similar to Task N — repeat the code" | `writing-plans` → "code that does appear is source, not illustration" | NARROWED | The transcription hazard stays true for code that does appear; the mandate that every code step carry a real body is what produced transcript plans (rows above). Cross-task reuse now goes through the Interfaces block. The row "A plan carrying real code runs about as long as its diff" keeps its anchor and loses its premise. |
| **REFUSED: adopt OpenSpec's structure** — a proposal, spec deltas, a design doc and tasks per change, with living specs synced to the code | — | REFUSED (switch accounts, four orbits) | Diego Fernando Rojas, "Why I stopped using OpenSpec" — https://dfrojas.com/software/why-i-stopped-using-openspec.html — "Keeping those documents synchronized eventually cost more than the persistent context they provided." HN — https://news.ycombinator.com/item?id=49734264 — an org adoption: "Every change, medium or larger, turns into a large set of multiple markdown documents … the spec almost immediately becomes out of date." Dex Horthy — https://x.com/dexhorthy/status/2090091396917248209 — keeping all specs in sync with all code "manufactured a new problem". Matt Pocock — https://x.com/mattpocockuk/status/2083563195671667176 — "Specs are a cached representation of the code". The content rules above transfer; the structure does not. Also REFUSED PHASES (c)(d). |
| **The kit's own A/B** | `writing-plans` | INTERNAL MEASUREMENT (harness not published; small n; outcome at ceiling) | Two fixtures built blind to both skill versions (a Python ledger and a TypeScript booking service; 19 hidden tests each — 12 stated, 4 implied, 3 house rules — validated: base scores 0, reference full, careless variants drop). Arms, two plans each per fixture: no skill, this skill before (cce6b62), after. 18 builds by Sonnet and Haiku executors, every one 19/19 — these fixtures do not separate the arms on outcome, so what remains is cost and plan quality. Planner means, before → after: 209k → 169k tokens, 1,021 → 717 s, 199 → 60 code lines, ~3,000 → ~2,800 words (no skill: 5,421 words, 487 code lines); Haiku executors spent ~13% more tokens on after-plans, writing the bodies themselves. A blind judge over anonymised plans, rubric fixed before reading (the fixture authors' planted traps): traps after 24/26, before 25/26, no skill 25/26; gaps 2 / 3 / 2; factual errors 0 / 0 / 2; code share 0–3% / 18–22% / 19–29%. The shipped text adds to the measured one a handful of sentences — high-level-is-not-thin, the settled-edit clause, one approach, constraints carrying their failure, the fixed-base check (the last two from the judge's findings) — and review fixes (tactic changes logged in `## Clarifications`; `## User Review Required` among the binding parts; the step-done criterion reworded to "without guessing what you decided"; the template commit staging every path it names); not re-run. Falsifier: a harder fixture on which after-plans lose hidden tests that before-plans pass. |
